创作直觉、打破规则与超越理性的创造力哲学 The Diary Of A CEO The Diary Of A CEO 2026-09-21

来源: The Diary Of A CEO

原文: 查看

发布时间: 2026-09-21

摘要:

文章探讨了艺术创作中的直觉性（如创作过程中的灵感）、打破既有规则的必要性以及在面对人生低谷时的风险承担。核心观点强调创造力需要打破束缚，并指出真正的洞察力往往来源于超越科学和理性的内在直觉与共时性，而非纯粹的数据分析。

创作的直觉与传奇艺术家的工作方式

Rick Rubin：Jay-Z 的创作方式与其他人截然不同。我合作过的所有其他说唱歌手都会花大量时间来写歌词，但我从未见过 Jay 动笔写过歌词。当我为他播放《99 Problems》的伴奏（beat）时，他说：“行，你就一直循环播放这首伴奏。”

Original English

Rick Rubin: Jay-Z wrote in a way that no one else wrote. All the other rappers that I worked with spent a lot of time writing lyrics. And I never saw Jay write lyrics. And when I played him the beat for 99 Problems, he said, "Okay, just keep playing it over and over again."

Rick Rubin：他就坐在房间的后方，你能听到他在那里喃喃自语或者轻声哼唱。过了二三十分钟，他就像是突然来了灵感：“好了，我搞定了。”然后他直接跑进录音室录制了第一段主歌（first verse）。他大概录了三遍（three takes），之后他走出来听回放，说：“哦，对，我觉得第二版就挺好。”这一切完全只发生在他的脑海之中。

Original English

Rick Rubin: And he sat in the back of the room. You could hear him mumbling or humming. After 20 or 30 minutes, he's like, "Okay, I got it." And ran into the other room and did the first verse. He did, let's say, three takes. And then he came in, he listened to him. He's like, "Oh, yeah. I think the second one's good." It just happens in his mind.

Steven Bartlett：看看与你合作过的这一长串人物名单——我是说，这份名单简直令人难以置信：Jay-Z、Kanye、Adele、Justin Timberlake、Mick Jagger，还有 Red Hot Chili Peppers……你认为这些人为什么会打电话找你合作？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: And then this big list of people that you've worked with. I mean, it's just this unbelievable list. Jay-Z and Kanye, Adele, Justin Timberlake, and Mick Jagger and the Red Hot Chili Peppers. Why do you think these people call you?

Rick Rubin：因为这 40 年来我一直都在创作事物，而且我最初做音乐完全只是出于个人爱好。最后它却自然而然地发展壮大，变成了一门事业。至于那份名单上的人，其实他们并没有什么与众不同之处。在我看来，他们只是做出了一种决定的普通人。我觉得 Eminem 就是个极好的例子：Eminem 是一个始终在不断写作的人，因为他渴望做到极致，尽可能成为最优秀的自己，他对这一点有着近乎疯狂的执念。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Because I've been making things for 40 years and I started making music as a hobby. It ended up taking on a life of its own and becoming a business. And that list, they're not special. I would say they're ordinary people who made a decision. I think of Eminem as a good example. Eminem is someone who's always writing because he wants to be the best he could possibly be and he's insanely obsessive about that.

打破规则与承担风险的创造力

Steven Bartlett：看起来似乎某些人生来就比其他人更具创造力。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: It appears that some people are just like inherently more creative than others.

Rick Rubin：其实创造力是在我们的一生中被逐渐消磨、扼杀掉的。我们去上学，被教育要遵守规则。然而规则的存在并不是为了帮助我们，规则的存在是为了控制我们。我们没有被培养成自由的创造性思考者。因此，我主张我们应该打破束缚，自由地表达自己。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Well, creativity is beaten out of us over the course of our lives. We go to school, we're taught to follow rules. The rules are not there to help us. The rules are there to control us. We're not taught to be free creative thinkers. So, I'm suggesting we break free and express ourselves freely.

Steven Bartlett：具体该怎么做呢？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: How?

Rick Rubin：所有最美好的事物都伴随着风险。只有通过冒险，伟大之处才能得以显现。因此，某些旧有的东西必须死去。在那个当下，那种生活方式、那条职业路径、那段人际关系，这些就是必须被彻底舍弃、走向消亡的东西。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Well, all of the best things involve risk. It's only through the risk that the greatness is shown. And so something needs to die. That lifestyle in that moment, that career path, that relationship, that's what needs to die.

走出人生的至暗时刻

Steven Bartlett：对于那些想要按下人生重启键的人来说，朝那个方向迈出的第一步是什么？在你三十出头的时候，你自己也曾经历过一段抑郁期。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Is there a step one in that direction for people that want to hit the reset button? In your early 30s, you had a depressive episode yourself.

Rick Rubin：是的，那确实是一段极其艰难的时光。我感觉自己脚下的地毯被猛然抽走了一样。我开始出现惊恐发作（panic attacks），整夜整夜无法入眠，病得很重，那种感觉就像是被一支毒箭射中了一样。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah. The first one I'll say is... Yeah. It was really a tough time. I felt like the rug had been pulled out from under me. Started having panic attacks and couldn't sleep and got really sick and it felt like I was shot with a poison arrow.

Steven Bartlett：带我走进你生命中的那个阶段吧。顺便提一个奇特的事实：现在观看这个视频的大多数人——大约 58% 的观众——其实还没有订阅这个频道。从统计学上看，很可能就包括现在的你。所以，我能请大家帮个小忙吗？如果这个频道曾经为你带来过任何价值，请帮我一个忙，点击订阅按钮。这不需要任何花费，但对我们的帮助超乎你的想象。正如大家所见，频道规模越大，我们就能在嘉宾邀请和制作水平上投入更多。非常感谢大家，希望大家喜欢本期节目。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Take me into that part of your life. So, um, here is a strange fact. Most of the people watching this right now, about 58% of you aren't yet subscribed to this channel. Statistically, that is probably you. So, could I ask you a small favor? If this channel has ever given you any value at all, please could you do me a favor and hit the subscribe button. It costs nothing. It helps us more than you could know. And the bigger the channel gets, as you've seen, the more we can invest in the guests and the production. So, thank you so much. And I hope you enjoy this episode.

超越理性的“不可抗辩”原则

Steven Bartlett：Rick，我想从我最感到好奇的地方开始探讨。在你的职业定义上，你通常是如何对自己进行归类或自我认同的？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: I'm going to start Rick where my curiosity is. Yes. How do you sort of self-categorize or self-identify in terms of like your profession?

Rick Rubin：嗯，我会说我是艺术和创造力领域的参与者。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I would say I'm involved in art and creativity.

Steven Bartlett：那么你在艺术与创造力中的具体参与方式是什么呢？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: And what is your involvement in art and creativity?

Rick Rubin：可能是一位指导教练（coach），也可能是一位合作者（collaborator）。它可能出现在一个电影项目中，也可能出现在某位初创公司创始人的身上——他们想要探讨针对某些问题有什么创造性的解决方案。我们面临的任何问题，或者任何我们想要提升和改进的事物，通常都会存在一条创造性的路径去优化，或者至少可以通过测试去看看是否有其他解决之道。通常情况下，人们普遍接受的前进方案，本身就是另一种局限。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Might be as a coach. It might be as a collaborator. It might come up in a film project or it might come up in someone who has a startup who wants to talk about what are the creative solutions. Any problem that we have or anything we want to get better at, there's usually a creative path to improve or at least to test to see if there are other ways around. Often the accepted version of how to move forward is another limitation.

Steven Bartlett：我对你在寻找解决方案时的原则非常好奇，无论是在商业领域还是在音乐领域。我非常想知道在寻找解决方案的过程中是否存在某些相似性，因为我自己是一名创业者，我想我也是一名播客主，我整天都在做投资。我始终在追求解决问题的方法——人际问题、创意问题。你是否有任何高维度的原则，是广泛适用且独立于具体所解决的问题类型的？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: I'm very curious about your principles as it relates to finding the solution, whether that's in business or whether it's in music. And I'm really interested in if there's any similarities in the process of finding a solution because I'm an entrepreneur. I guess I'm a podcaster as well. I do investments all day every day. I'm in the pursuit of a solution to a problem, a people problem, a creative problem. Do you have any high-level principles that are broadly applicable irrespective of the type of problem you're solving?

Rick Rubin：我可以跟你分享一个非常有趣的原则。我最近一直在思考的一个词叫做“不可抗辩 / 无法辩护”（indefensible）。“Indefensible” 是一个很有意思的词，因为它是非常贬义、非常强烈的贬义词。如果某件事是 indefensible 的，它不仅仅代表糟糕，它意味着极其极端。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I'll tell you an interesting one. A word I've been thinking about lately is indefensible. Indefensible is a funny word because it's a really pejorative word. It's a strong pejorative. If something is indefensible, it's not just bad. Indefensible is really extreme.

Rick Rubin：但我认为在艺术创作中，“不可抗辩”其实是一件好事。也许不仅是一件好事，甚至可能是一件必要的事：你把事物推向了一种极端，以至于它是无法用逻辑去辩护的。我无法向你解释为什么，我无法给出理由，因为这已经超越了理性的范畴。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I'm thinking that in making art, indefensible is actually a good thing. Maybe not only a good thing, maybe a necessary thing. That you take things to the extreme that are indefensible. I can't tell you why. I can't tell you why because it's beyond reason.

Rick Rubin：所以我属于这样一个阵营：理性在决策过程中只占极小的一小部分。我更倾向于直觉的阵营，或者说是某种来自宇宙的指引。保持开放的心态，去接受更宏大事物的指引——接受宇宙中那股创造性力量的引导。你可以把它称作上帝（God），我自己就称它为上帝，但你并不一定要这么称呼它。

Original English

Rick Rubin: So, I'm in that camp of reason is a tiny sliver of how decisions get made. And I would be much more in the camp of intuition or maybe some sort of a guidance from the universe. Openness to being guided by something more, the creative force of the universe. You could call it God. I call it God. You're not required to.

“氛围编程”与回应宇宙的邀请

Steven Bartlett：在你参与创作的艺术作品中，有哪些是你当时会定义为“不可抗辩”的？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: What art that you've been involved in creating would you define in the moment as being indefensible?

Rick Rubin：一年多以前，网络上出现了一个关于“氛围编程”（vibe coding）的梗（meme）。整件事的起因是一件新鲜事：一位名叫 Andrej Karpathy 的人创造了“vibe coding”这个词。当时我根本不知道这是什么。就在他提出这个词并发布推特的那一天，有人做了一张图：把我戴着耳机、闭着眼睛、手握鼠标的照片和 vibe coding 关联了起来。那张图片迅速成为了 vibe coding 的象征。

Original English

Rick Rubin: A little over a year ago there was a meme about vibe coding. The way it started was if something new happened. A guy named Andrej Karpathy coined the term vibe coding. I didn't know what it was. That same day that he coined the term and tweeted about it, someone put a picture of me wearing headphones with my eyes closed, hand on a mouse. And that image became associated with vibe coding.

Rick Rubin：这原本跟我毫无关系。我看到了它，但不知道它代表什么意思。然而在接下来的几天里，朋友们开始从各种渠道向我发送成百上千张这张与 vibe coding 关联的照片。我心想：“嗯……如果是以前那个还没有写出《创造力的修行》（The Creative Act）的我，肯定会一笑了之，然后继续过自己的生活。”

Original English

Rick Rubin: Had nothing to do with me. I saw it. I didn't know what it meant. But then over the next few days, friends started sending me hundreds of this image associated with vibe coding in all different ways. And I thought, hm, now the old me, pre-Creative Act me would have laughed and moved on with my life.

Rick Rubin：而在创作《创造力的修行》的过程中我学到的一件事——书中也反复谈到的一点——就是我们周围时刻都在接收着各种信息，身边时刻都存在着各种各样的契机或邀请。换作过去，如果有人跟我推荐一部听起来我并不想看的电影，觉得“这不适合我”，我就会直接忽略它。但如果有两三个人都对我说“你或许应该看看这部电影”，哪怕我原本不感兴趣，这几乎就像是宇宙在这件事背后注入了过多的能量。这正是一个有大量能量朝我涌来的例子，所有能量都与 vibe coding 相关，而我当时甚至不知道它到底是什么。

Original English

Rick Rubin: And one of the things that I learned through working on The Creative Act, one of the things it talks about a lot is we're given information all the time around us. There are all these opportunities or invitations being made all the time. In the past, if someone told me about a movie that sounded like I didn't want to see it, that doesn't sound like it's for me, I would ignore it. But if two or three people say you might want to see this movie, even if it's not interesting to me, it's almost like the universe, too much energy behind this. So this was an example of a lot of energy coming towards me relating to vibe coding. I don't know what it is.

Rick Rubin：于是我想：“好吧，我该如何参与其中呢？如果我想参与到这件正在发生的事情中——作为一个实验、一个创意的实验。”我想：“好吧，我要写一条关于 vibe coding 的搞笑推文。”我想了想，写下了这样一句话：“工具来来去去，唯有氛围程序员永存（Tools will come, tools will go, only the vibe coder remains）。”通常我的推文大概也就一两万的浏览量，结果第二天我一看，这条搞笑推文竟然有 150 万的浏览量！

Original English

Rick Rubin: And I thought, okay, how do I participate? If I want to participate in this thing that's going on, an experiment, creative experiment. And I thought, okay, I'm going to write a joke tweet related to vibe coding. I thought about it and came up with tools will come, tools will go, only the vibe coder remains. And typically the tweets get 10,000, 20,000 views. I look the next day and this joke tweet has a million and a half views.

Rick Rubin：这就像是有什么事情正在发生。那完全是一次“不可抗辩”的举动——我没有任何理性理由去那么做。但我感受到了从各个方向朝我涌来的这股能量，于是我参与了进去，我回应了这个邀请。反馈随之而来，大家都非常感兴趣。我想：“嗯，下一步该怎么做呢？”下一步就是：“好吧，我其实依然不太懂什么是 vibe coding，也许我可以写一本关于 vibe coding 的书。”于是我把我所有正经的工作、专业上的事务全都搁置一旁，全身心投入到这本关于 vibe coding 的书中。

Original English

Rick Rubin: It's like something's going on. It was an indefensible act. There was no reason for me to do it. But I'm feeling this energy coming at me from all of these different directions. I participate. I answer the invitation. Comes back, there's interest. Said, hm, what would be the next step? And the next step is, well, I still don't really know what vibe coding is, maybe I'll write a book about vibe coding. I put aside all of my real work, my professional work to dedicate myself to this book on vibe coding.

Rick Rubin：同样，我无法给出解释，我无法告诉你为什么，我也不能说这是一个好主意——完全不是因为这些。我只是在追随一种内心的召唤，感觉自己仿佛被邀请参与到这件事情中来，而这就是我回应召唤的方式。所以我就这么做了。

Original English

Rick Rubin: And again, I couldn't explain it. I couldn't tell you why. I can't say it was a good idea, none of those things. I was following a calling, feeling like I was being invited to participate in this thing, and this is the way that I could answer the call. So I did.

挣脱既有标签的束缚

Steven Bartlett：在我们生活的许多层面，我们都可能收到参与某件看似与我们“毫不相干”之事的邀请。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: In so many parts of our life, we might get that invitation to participate in something that is none of our business.

Rick Rubin：是的，时时刻刻都会发生。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yes. All the time.

Steven Bartlett：没错。而我们通常都会选择拒绝，因为那不符合我们名片上的头衔，不是我们过去所做的事情。而且你知道，我们变得越成功，我们在许多方面反而变得越发狭隘。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Yeah. And we usually say no because it's not what's on our business card. It's not what we've done in the past. And we are, you know, the more successful we become, the more narrow we become in many respects.

Rick Rubin：是的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yes.

Steven Bartlett：我正在思考这样一个观点：一个人究竟该如何抗拒贴在自己身上的标签，从而能够抓住所有的召唤，或者说，不至于错失……

Original English

Steven Bartlett: I'm thinking about this idea of like how one resists their label so they can capitalize on all the callings or you know, so they don't miss...

Rick Rubin：对，这很重要。任何你贴在自己身上的标签，或者别人贴在你身上的……

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah, important. Any label that you put on yourself or that's put...

摆脱标签与追随“无法辩护”的直觉

Rick Rubin: 强加于你的东西会让你变得狭隘。不要依赖标签，我们早已超越了标签。

Original English

Rick Rubin: ...on you makes you smaller. No label supply. No label supply. We are beyond labels.

Interviewer: 但贴标签确实很诱人，不是吗？因为在酒会社交时，标签是非常好用的省事捷径。

Original English

Interviewer: It's tempting though, isn't it? Because labels are nice shortcuts at cocktail parties.

Rick Rubin: 这里同样没有捷径。我希望你不要花太多时间去参加那些互相评头论足的酒会。

Original English

Rick Rubin: There are also no shortcuts, and I hope you don't spend too... and shaming cocktail parties.

Interviewer: 不，我没有，别担心。我只是在推测那里会发生什么。

Original English

Interviewer: No, I don't. Don't worry. I'm guessing what goes on there.

Interviewer: 好吧，这很有意思——这种有时去追随“无法辩护”（indefensible）之物的想法。

Original English

Interviewer: Okay. So, that's interesting. This idea of sometimes following the indefensible.

Rick Rubin: 只要你感受到了某种召唤，即便它在逻辑上讲不通。如果一位艺术家去创作一首歌，而你真真切切地觉得：“这对我来说意义非凡。我认为可能不会有人喜欢它，但我内心里深深被它触动，尽管我无法用逻辑去为它辩护。”——那就是你能做到的最好的事。

如果你做出来的东西是那种心想“这是面向所有人的，大家一定会喜欢它”的作品，那就未免太轻而易举了。最有趣的事物往往是具有挑战性的：它挑战你自己，也挑战那些看到它的人。那些真正具有颠覆性、革命性的作品，大部分我在一生中爱上的艺术与创意作品，刚看到或刚听到时，我一开始可能并不喜欢，因为信息量太庞大，或者我完全没有任何认知语境来理解它。真正具有革命性的作品，我们一开始都是缺乏语境去理解的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Well, if you feel a call, even if it doesn't make sense... If you, for an artist to write a song and you really feel like, this is really meaningful to me. I think no one's going to like it, but I really feel it. I can't defend it. That's the best thing you can do.

If you make something where you think, "This is for everyone or they're going to love it," it's too easy. The most interesting things are challenging. It's challenging to you. It's challenging to the people who see it. The things that are revolutionary. Most of the things that I've fallen in love with over my life creatively, art, when I first see it often or first hear it, I might not like it at first because it's too much or I don't have any context for it. The true revolutionary works, we don't have context for.

商业数字与打破范式：《Yeezus》与《Kid A》

Interviewer: Rick，在我看来，有时那些千篇一律、延续套路的作品反而能斩获更大的商业数字。所以有一段时间我一直在思考两位艺术家。其中一位是 Drake，听着，他做出了极棒的音乐，制造爆款金曲，人人都喜欢，新歌一出我们就在俱乐部里跟着跳舞。然后我又想到了你和 Kanye 合作的一张唱片——《Yeezus》。

Original English

Interviewer: It appears to me, Rick, that sometimes things that are more of the same do bigger numbers. So I've been thinking about maybe for a period of time I was thinking about two artists. One of them is Drake who, listen, makes great music, makes hits, everybody loves them. They come out, we dance in the clubs to them, etc. Then I was thinking about, I think a record that you worked on with Kanye, Yeezus.

Rick Rubin: 嗯。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Mhm.

Interviewer: 我至今还记得作为 Kanye 的狂热粉丝，第一次听到那张专辑时我人在哪里。我当时心想：“太棒了，又要来一张《The College Dropout》式的神作了！”我放上唱片一听，整个人都懵了：“这到底是个什么玩意儿？”

Original English

Interviewer: And I remember where I was when I first heard that as a huge Kanye fan. I thought, here we go. I'm going to get more College Dropout. Here we go. And I put it on and I was like, what the... is this?

Interviewer: 但后来我爱死它了。在我们六个人的小圈子里……

Original English

Interviewer: But then I loved it. And in my little group of six...

Rick Rubin: 你花了多久才喜欢上它？

Original English

Rick Rubin: How long did it take?

Interviewer: 过了好一阵子。

Original English

Interviewer: A while.

Rick Rubin: 确实需要一阵子。

Original English

Rick Rubin: A while.

Interviewer: 确实花了不少时间。我的意思是，像 Drake 的作品，我往往初听就很喜欢；但面对《Yeezus》那张专辑，我必须真正耐着性子去多听、去坚持，然后我才意识到那是天才之作，因为那是一个全新的范式。你参与制作了那张专辑。

Original English

Interviewer: It took a while. I mean, you know, with Drake's stuff, I tend to like it on first listen, but with that Yeezus album, I had to really persevere, and then I thought it was genius because it was a new paradigm. Um, you worked on that album.

Rick Rubin: 是的。那正是一个绝佳的例子：它在某种意义上是一张“反嘻哈”（anti-hip hop）的专辑，而且这是蓄意为之的。毫无疑问，我们当时尽可能深地踏入了一个“无法辩护”的未知领域。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah. And that's a good example of like, it was a sort of an anti-hip hop album, and that was done intentionally. And it definitely... we went as far as we could into an indefensible realm.

Interviewer: 但数据指标呢？销量怎么办？唱片公司那边怎么交代？

Original English

Interviewer: But what about the numbers? What about the sales? What about the record label?

Rick Rubin: 对现在的你而言，它是一张重要的专辑吗？

Original English

Rick Rubin: Is it an important album to you now?

Interviewer: 是的。

Original English

Interviewer: Yeah.

Rick Rubin: 好吧，那它的使命就达成了。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Okay. Did its job.

短期利益与艺术表达：日记本的比喻与林肯公园的转型

Interviewer: 但是那些短期利益驱动呢？人们会说：“听着，我们需要 Drake，我们需要你再做出一首那样的金曲。”

Original English

Interviewer: Those near-term incentives though. Listen, we need Drake. We need you to make another...

Rick Rubin: 我们在任何时候都绝不考虑短期利益驱动。从来没有过，哪怕一次都没有考虑过，这种考量根本不存在。我们在创造能够流传永恒的作品，在创造对我们而言真正发自内心的私人表达。

如果这是切切实实发自个人内心的东西，如果有人不理解，那仅仅是因为他们目前还没理解，或者也许他们永远也不会理解。而这完全没关系，因为我知道这是我的“日记本记录”。别人能读了你的日记本，然后跑来告诉你“你写错了”、“这不够好”或者“这不是真的”吗？他们不能，因为那是你看世界的方式。

我们创作的每一样东西，都是一篇日记。我们所创造的一切，都是我们个人真实的自我表达。我们把它表达出来的目的，是为了向你展示：你看待世界是那样的角度，而我们——或者说我——是这样看待世界的。有了这份视角之后，你就会领会到：“啊，我以前从未从这个角度思考过。原来世界不仅如此，世界比我想象的还要广阔。”世界不应该只是更多千篇一律的东西，不应该只是追逐更大的数字。

而且，追逐数字的游戏本质上是一个更短期的游戏。我记得我和林肯公园（Linkin Park）合作制作过一张专辑。在当时，他们可以说是全世界最顶尖的乐队之一，此前他们已经发了三张在风格上极为相似的专辑。那是说唱摇滚（rap rock），当时掀起了一场说唱摇滚浪潮——由 Rage Against the Machine 开创先河，接着是 Korn、Limp Bizkit 等等所有这些乐队。林肯公园大概是那股音乐浪潮最后的巅峰代表，他们在那种风格上取得了巨大的成功。

当我们开始合作时，面临着一个抉择：我们既可以继续顺水推舟做一张同样风格的新专辑，听众必然会买账，制作起来也轻车熟路；或者，我们可以忠于乐队在成立大约 8 到 10 年后的当下心境，不受拘束地去寻找全新的声音、下一个声音。当我们最终选择变革时，大约有一半的老听众不喜欢它，但另一半听众爱极了它，并且吸引来了全新的受众群。这让他们奠定了更长久的职业生涯，能够持续做他们真正想做的音乐。而如果他们当时在那个流派末期再做一张老套路专辑，也许短期内依然会像过去一样成功，但从长远来看，那个流派本身已经在走向消亡。

Original English

Rick Rubin: We never consider near-term incentive ever at any point in time. Never once. Never a consideration. It doesn't exist. We're making things forever. We're making things that are truly personal to us. And if it's truly personal to us, if someone doesn't get it, that's they just don't get it yet. Or maybe they'll never get it. And it's fine because I know this is my diary entry. Can someone else read your diary entry and tell you it's wrong, it's not good enough, or it's not true? It's not how you see the world.

Everything we make is a diary entry. Everything we make is our personal true expression. The reason we're saying it is to show you see it like that. We see it like this or I see it like this. This is how I see it. And with that information, you can see it's like, ah, I never considered that before. There's more. The world is bigger than I thought. It's not just more the same. It's not just more numbers.

And it's also the... the more numbers game is a shorter term game. I remember I made an album with Linkin Park who at the time were probably one of the biggest bands in the world and they had made three albums that were I would say stylistically very similar. They were rap rock and they were... there was this... there was a rap rock movement. Korn, I guess. Rage Against the Machine was first, Korn, Limp Bizkit, all these bands. And Linkin Park was probably the last of that wave of music. And they were wildly successful doing that music.

And when we started working together, there was a choice. We could continue and do the next in that same style which the audience would love and it would be easy to do, or we could be true to where the band was in this moment of time, 10 years after they started, about probably 8, 10 years after they started, and be free to find the new sound, the next sound. And when we did that, probably half of the audience didn't like it and half of the audience loved it, and new people came along, and they set themselves up to have a much longer career of making the things that they wanted to make. Whereas they were already on the tail end of a genre that had they made another one, maybe it would have been... it probably would have been, you know, as successful as what they had done. But long-term the genre was dying.

Interviewer: 嗯。

Original English

Interviewer: Mhm.

Rick Rubin: 同样，在做决策的当下并没有去计较这些得失。我想到的另一个例子是 Radiohead 的《Kid A》。我还记得当《Kid A》刚发行时，我一点也不喜欢它。我当时深爱《The Bends》和《OK Computer》，他们是我最爱的乐队。然后他们做出了这张《Kid A》，彻底改头换面，这在一开始真的让我感到被疏离和劝退。然而到了今天，它可能是我最喜欢的一张 Radiohead 专辑。这感觉就像是一场冒险——是的，但所有最伟大的事物都伴随着风险。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Again, none of this was thought about at the time. Another example I think of is Radiohead's Kid A. I remember when Kid A came out, I didn't like it at all. I loved The Bends and OK Computer. They were my favorite band. And then they made this album Kid A. And it was totally different and it really alienated me. And today that might be my favorite Radiohead album. It feels like a risk. Yes. But that's... all of the best things involve risk.

艺术博弈 vs 商业投资

Interviewer: 因为我敢肯定你也见过很多反例：有人践行了这种理念，坚持做自己想做的、只为自己创作而不去过多考虑受众，但结果彻底失败了。

Original English

Interviewer: Because I'm sure you've also got examples where someone followed that thesis of listening to what they wanted to make or doing it for themselves and not thinking too much about the audience and it didn't work.

Rick Rubin: 当然有。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Of course.

Interviewer: 他们本来完全可以照着套路做出一首热门单曲，然后把房贷还清……

Original English

Interviewer: And they could have just made, you know, a hit and paid off their mortgage and...

Rick Rubin: 是的，但这没那么有意思。我知道这对我来说索然无味。我认为那是完全不同的两场游戏：那是商业投资的游戏，而我身处的是艺术的游戏，纯粹是截然不同的两码事。另一种商业游戏并没有错，只是那不是我的赛道。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah, it's not so interesting. I know it's not so interesting to me. I think that's... it's a different game. That's the, you know, that's the investment commerce game and I'm in the art game. It's just a different thing. There's nothing wrong with that other game. It's just not my game.

Interviewer: 从长远来看，哪一种模式能带来更大的成功？假设在这个语境下，我们把“成功”定义为人们主观认可的好口碑以及赚到的金钱收益。

Original English

Interviewer: Which one leads to more success over the long term? Defined by, let's say, if we define success in this context, by the subjective opinion of people that that was good and money.

Rick Rubin: 我只能说，我已经从事创作整整 40 年了，我从来没有按照你所说的那种迎合模式去做过。我一直都在承担风险，我一直都在做我最钟爱的东西，我一直都在支持艺术家去创造他们自己最钟爱的东西。幸运的是，由此诞生了许多具有革命性的作品；而且我认为，这些作品或许也激励了其他艺术家变得更加自由、更加真实、更加坦诚，敢于去冒更大的风险做出更有趣的东西，让这个世界变得更加有趣。

Original English

Rick Rubin: All I can say is that I've been making things for 40 years. I have never done it the way you're suggesting. I have always taken the risk. I've always made my favorite thing. I've always supported the artists in making their favorite thing, and luckily a lot of revolutionary things have happened from that, and I would say things that probably inspired other artists to be more free and more true and more honest and take bigger risks to make something more interesting, made the world more interesting place.

与 Jay-Z 合作《99 Problems》的幕后

Interviewer: 你认为为什么这些顶尖艺术家会打电话找你？我想不同艺术家可能有不同的原因。Jay-Z 当时为什么找你？

Original English

Interviewer: Why do you think these people call you? I think it's probably different for different artists. Why did Jay-Z call you?

Rick Rubin: 我第一次见到 Jay 是在他制作《The Black Album》的时候。他当时正打算退休，这张唱片原本将作为他的天鹅之歌——他的告别专辑。《The Black Album》本该是 Jay-Z 的收官之作，虽然后来事实并非如此，但当时确实是这么计划的。他邀请了他最喜欢的 10 位制作人，每人跟他合作一首单曲。我们合作的那首叫作《99 Problems》，那是一次非常棒、非常有趣的创作经历。

Original English

Rick Rubin: The time that I first met Jay was when he was making The Black Album and he wanted to... he was planning on retiring and this was going to be his swan song. His final album. The Black Album was the Jay-Z's last album. Turned out not to be, but that was the plan. And he asked his 10 favorite producers to each make one track with them. And ours was called 99 Problems. And it was a really fun experience to make.

Interviewer: 那真是一张标志性的传奇单曲。你在那首作品里的贡献是什么？毕竟人人都知道那首歌。

Original English

Interviewer: I mean, what an iconic record that is. What was your contribution to that record? Everybody knows that record.

Rick Rubin: 人人皆知。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Everybody.

Interviewer: 是的。

Original English

Interviewer: Yeah.

Rick Rubin: 我们制作了伴奏音轨，放给 Jay 听，他很喜欢，随后便填词并在上面完成了说唱。

Original English

Rick Rubin: We created the track and we played it for Jay and he liked it and then he wrote the words and rapped on top of it.

Interviewer: 所以你负责了编曲制作。

Original English

Interviewer: So you produced the music.

Rick Rubin: 制作了音乐。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Made the music.

Interviewer: 我听说你在歌词构思方面也给了他指导。

Original English

Interviewer: I read that you gave him direction in terms of the lyrics as well.

Rick Rubin: 当时我的朋友 Chris Rock 提出了一个想法：Ice-T 曾经有一首歌叫《99 Problems》，但主题正好相反，讲的是“我有 99 个麻烦但女人不是其中之一”，通篇是一个关于女孩们的故事。

Original English

Rick Rubin: When my friend Chris Rock had the idea of... Ice-T had a song called 99 Problems that was the opposite. It was "I have 99 problems and a ... ain't one", and then it's a story about the girls.

Interviewer: 嗯。

Original English

Interviewer: Mhm.

Rick Rubin: Chris 说：“你知道吗，‘99 Problems’会是一个绝妙的副歌 Hook，而且它完全没必要写成关于女孩的内容。”于是我向 Jay 建议：不如把它写成真正关于生活中各种“麻烦与问题”的歌。后来这首歌就应运而生了。

Original English

Rick Rubin: And Chris said, you know, "99 Problems" would be a great hook and it doesn't have to be about the girls. And then I suggested to Jay, make it about the problems. And then that's what it was.

Interviewer: 商界人士、初创企业也会向你寻求建议，企业家们也是如此？

Original English

Interviewer: And business people, startups, they seek your advice as well. Entrepreneurs...

Rick Rubin: 经常找我。

Original English

Rick Rubin: All the time.

Interviewer: 那他们寻求的是什么呢？

Original English

Interviewer: And what are they seeking?

Rick Rubin: 我们先多聊聊《99 Problems》吧，因为这背后有一个很有趣的故事。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Let's talk about 99 Problems a little more because it's an interesting story.

Interviewer: 好的。

Original English

Interviewer: Sure.

Rick Rubin: Jay-Z 当时……

Original English

Rick Rubin: Jay-Z was...

打破行业陈规：不随波逐流的音乐力量

Rick Rubin：……签约到了 Def Jam 唱片公司。Def Jam 是我创立的厂牌。当我们制作《99 Problems》这首歌时，我已经不在 Def Jam 了。我记得把这首歌拿给 Def Jam 的人听，当时 Def Jam 的总裁听完后说：“哦，这太棒了。它绝对成不了单曲，但会是一首很棒的专辑曲目。”我记得当时心想：好吧。再说一次，我并不确定，我也无法断言。但我创立的唱片公司的现任总裁却说这首歌绝不可能成为单曲，原因仅仅是它听起来和电台里的其他流行歌曲都不一样。

Original English

Rick Rubin: ...signed to Def Jam Records. Def Jam Records was the label that I founded, and I was no longer at Def Jam when we made "99 Problems." And I remember giving it to the people at Def Jam, and the person who was the president of Def Jam at the time listened to it and he's like, "Oh, this is great. It'll never be a single, but it'll be a great album track." I remember thinking, okay, and again, I don't know, I couldn't say, but the president of the label that I founded was saying this could never be a single because it doesn't sound like everything else on the radio.

Rick Rubin：那是过去的思维模式，也是一种常规的套路——你制作去电台播放的内容，就必须迎合电台，听起来和电台里的所有东西一模一样。但如果你真的那样做，谁会在乎呢？那不过是千篇一律的复制品而已。所以《99 Problems》听起来和电台里的任何歌都截然不同。因此当它最终登上电台时，产生了极其巨大的轰动与反响。

Original English

Rick Rubin: That was the old way of thinking. That was the normal way of thinking, of like you make things to go on the radio that sound like everything else on the radio. But if you do that, who cares? It's just more of the same. So "99 Problems" didn't sound like anything on the radio. So when it eventually got on the radio, it had a big impact.

Rick Rubin：我合作过的另一支乐队叫做“堕落体制”（System of a Down）。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Another band I work with is called System of a Down.

Steven Bartlett：是的，我记得。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Yeah, I remember.

Rick Rubin：我记得当时把他们的唱片带给 K-Rock 电台的凯文·韦瑟利（Kevin Weatherly）。K-Rock 是加州首屈一指的摇滚兼另类摇滚电台。只要他们愿意播一首歌，全美所有的其他另类摇滚电台都会跟进播放，他们就像是行业里的风向标电台。当时这支加州本土乐队刚刚开始在俱乐部里小有名气，可能也就两百人来看演出，但只要在俱乐部演，门票总能售罄，这是个很好的兆头。我带去的那首歌好像叫《Sugar》。我把歌带给凯文·韦瑟利，放给他听，结果他说：“我们不仅不会放这张唱片，而且我们电台永远都不会播这支乐队的歌。”因为在他听来那声音太疯狂了，跟电台里的任何东西都格格不入。

Original English

Rick Rubin: And I remember bringing their record to Kevin Weatherly at K-Rock, which is the rock station, alternative rock station in California. And if they play it, all of the other alternative rock stations in the country play it. They were like the leading station. And this was a California band that was starting to get popular in the clubs. Maybe 200 people would come, but in a club, the club would sell out. Good sign. And I brought—I think the song was called "Sugar"—and I brought it to Kevin Weatherly and I played it for him and he said, "Not only will we not play this record, we will never play this band on our station," because it sounded insane. It didn't sound like anything else on the station.

Rick Rubin：一年之后，这首歌成了该电台排名第一、点播量最高的冠军单曲。就在两周前，我亲眼看到“堕落体制”在巴黎的一座体育场里场场爆满，八万个座位连开两晚全部售罄。我无法向你确切解释这究竟是怎样或者为何奏效的，我也不能说有什么固定的公式可以遵循，但我可以说的是：我们只做我们发自内心热爱的东西。我们从来不去质疑别人是否会喜欢它，从不去琢磨听众会怎么想，也从不去考虑它在市场中处于什么定位。我们完全没有那些常规的考量。我们只是创造出我们自己最钟爱的作品，并将它推向一种无可辩驳、极致深入的境地，尽我们所能做到极致，而幸运的是，结果证明它是奏效的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: One year later, number one song on the station, most requested, and two weeks ago, I saw System of a Down sell out 80,000 seats in a stadium in Paris. They sold it out two nights in a row. I can't tell you how or why this works. I can't tell you that there's anything to follow, but I will say we made the things that we loved. We never questioned if anyone else was going to like it. We never questioned what the audience was going to think. We never questioned where does it fit in the market. None of those normal conversations. We made our favorite thing. We took it to an indefensible level as far as we could go, and luckily it worked out.

超觉冥想：向内探寻创造力的源泉

Steven Bartlett：有没有什么方法能够让人更清晰地了解自己究竟热爱什么？这听起来可能像个奇怪的问题，但是……

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Is there a way to get clearer on what you love? This sounds like a strange question, but um...

Rick Rubin：去冥想。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Meditate.

Steven Bartlett：冥想。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Meditate.

Rick Rubin：我在14岁的时候学会了冥想。我认为拥有 TM 冥想作为我生命中的定海神针和立足之本……

Original English

Rick Rubin: I learned to meditate when I was 14. And I think having a TM as a grounding element in my life...

Steven Bartlett：TM 是指？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: TM?

Rick Rubin：超觉冥想（Transcendental Meditation）。这是我14岁时学的第一种冥想方式，它彻底改变了我。我之所以深知这一点，是因为读大学时我停止了冥想；而大学毕业后我搬到加州，又重新开始了冥想。在内心深处，我知道这是一个重大决定，也知道总会有一个合适的契机重新开始。在中断了数年之后，我花了几个月时间才真正重新坐下来进行第一次冥想。就在那次初次恢复的冥想中，我猛然意识到：哦，这种感觉对我来说太熟悉了。即使我已经有五年没有练习，它依然是我人格中不可分割的重要组成部分，深刻塑造了我看待世界的方式。能够在年少时就获得这样的体验，对我影响至深。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Transcendental Meditation. That was the first type of meditation I learned when I was 14. It changed me. I know this because I stopped meditating when I went to college. And then when I moved to California, right after college, I started meditating again. Somewhere I knew it was a big decision and somewhere I knew there would be a right moment to start. And it took several months before I actually sat for the first meditation after not doing it for several years. And in that first meditation, I realized, oh, this is very familiar to me. This is a big part of who I am, even though I haven't done it in 5 years. It's a big part of how I see the world. The fact that I got to do that from a young age...

Steven Bartlett：为了理解拥有它时的你是怎样的，我想问你，如果没有它，你会成为什么样的人？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: To understand who you are with it, I'm going to ask you who you would be without it.

Rick Rubin：我不知道。那会……其实我也很好奇。但我很庆幸自己不用知道，因为我很欣慰自己拥有这项习惯。无论我眼下是否持续保持练习，在我的一生中我已经积累了足够的沉淀。当我冥想得更多时，这种体验会变得更深邃；但即使我不冥想，正如超觉冥想的导师玛哈礼希·玛赫西·优济（Maharishi Mahesh Yogi）所说，每次冥想都像是在银行里存钱，它会不断累积。以前这对我来说或许只是说辞，但在我切身的体验中，我真切地体会到这绝非说辞——我切实体会到了这种经历，它彻底改变了我观察世界的方式。我想说，你能借此看穿表面，洞察底层的深邃。对我而言，浮于表面的东西并没有那么有趣，我真正感兴趣的是隐藏在其背后的深度。

Steven Bartlett：那么我的第一个问题是，究竟什么是超觉冥想？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: So my first question would be what is Transcendental Meditation?

Rick Rubin：这是一种在心中默念真言（Mantra）的修习法。你需要闭上双眼静坐进行。你需要向专门的超觉冥想导师学习，我无法直接传授给你。我们闭上眼睛，然后真言会作为一种声音振动赋予给你。我是在14岁时获得属于我的真言的。自那时起，我从未大声念出过它，也从未向任何人透露过，这也是训练的一部分。它就像是一个专属的私密声音，你需要按照受训教导的方式与这个声音互动。每天至少练习一次，通常是两次，每次20分钟。我一般会在早晨刚醒来时做一次，然后在晚餐前再做一次，这就是两个常规的时间窗口。以前我住在加州并在不同的录音棚工作时，我曾与汤姆·佩蒂（Tom Petty）、约翰尼·卡什（Johnny Cash）以及不同的艺术家在录音开工前一起冥想，这成了我们工作习惯的一部分。我们和红辣椒乐队（Red Hot Chili Peppers）在录音前也会一起坐下来冥想。因此这种体验贯穿了我人生相当长的一段时间，它或许是我能提及的最为深刻的一件事。我们之后还会聊到其他事物，但这确实是一项可以习得、可以付诸实践，并真正重塑了自我的行动。

Original English

Rick Rubin: It's a mantra practice that's done silently. You do it sitting with your eyes closed. You learn it from a TM teacher. I can't teach it to you. We close our eyes and then the mantra is a sound vibration that gets given to you. I was given mine when I was 14. I have never uttered it out loud since that time. I've never shared it with anyone. When I lived in California and I was working in different recording studios, I meditated with Tom Petty and Johnny Cash and different artists before sessions; that would be part of our practice. Red Hot Chili Peppers—we would sit and meditate together before sessions. So, I've had this experience for a while in my life and it's probably the most profound thing I can mention. 商业逆向推导与内心信念的坚持

Rick Rubin：……放给你的朋友听，然后心想：“哇，你能相信世上竟然有这样的东西存在吗？这太酷了，不是吗？”

我试图做出的区分在于：通常，如果你读商业书籍或创业类书籍，当他们谈论创新、谈论像亚马逊这样伟大的公司时——或者前几天我听 Klarna 的 CEO 兼科技创始人 Sebastian 讲起——他们会花大量时间去思考客户、思考客户面临的问题，他们称之为“从答案逆向推导（work backwards）”。因此，他们会聚焦于某个人（比如 Rick）的世界，说：“Rick 遇到了这些困难，他处于某种困境……”然后他们会以此为起点，逆向推导出一个解决方案。亚马逊声称他们就是通过这种方式构建起整个平台的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: ...playing for your friend like, "Wow, can you believe something like this exists in the world? How cool is that?"

The distinction that I'm trying to make is often if you read business books or entrepreneurship books, when they're talking about innovation, when they're talking about the great companies like Amazon—or I heard Sebastian, who's the CEO of Klarna, say it the other day, who's a tech founder—they spend a lot of time thinking about the customer, the customer's problem, and they work backwards, they call it, to the answer. Steven Bartlett：杰夫·贝佐斯（Jeff Bezos）有一句非常著名的话，他说他把赌注押在那些他知道永远不会改变的事情上。他说：“未来十年里，绝不会有人跑来对我说：‘我希望我的包裹送得更慢一点。’”所以他可以全力以赴去加倍投入，解决用户的这个核心问题。这与你所说的观点是相互矛盾的，还是殊途同归？抑或是代表了两种截然不同性质的工作？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: And and one of the quotes that's quite famous with Jeff Bezos is he bets on things that he knows won't change. Rick Rubin：嗯，首先我想说的是，如果你回顾亚马逊的发展史，它大概连续亏损了将近二十年。在扭亏为盈之前，它一直被视作最糟糕的投资标的之一。但他们始终坚持加倍投入自己所坚信的东西。当时商业模式看起来并不奏效，受众反馈证明它不行，现有的商业模型也显示它走不通，但他们依然坚守自己所相信的方向，最终改变了整个世界。

制度扼杀与人人皆有的创造力

Rick Rubin：我认为，创造力是在我们漫长的成长岁月中被硬生生消磨、剥夺掉的。我们走进学校，被要求机械地重复别人告诉我们的标准答案；我们被规训整天端坐在椅子上一动不动；我们的灵性被各种体制和机构彻底压垮。

学校没有教会我们成为自由奔放的创意思考者，相反，我们被训练去墨守成规。而那些规则的存在并不是为了帮助我们成长，规则的存在是为了规训和控制我们。

我主张的是，我们应当打破这种桎梏，重获自由，自由自在地表达真实的自我。因为我们来到这个星球上的使命和意义，正是为了自由地表达我们自己，这就是我们存在的真正意义。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I think that creativity is beaten out of us over the course of our lives. I think we we go to school, we're told to repeat back what we're told. We're told to sit in a chair all day. Where our spirits are broken by institutions. We're not taught to be free creative thinkers. We're taught to follow rules. The rules are not there to help us. The rules are there to control us.

I'm suggesting we break free and be free and express ourselves freely. And that's our purpose here on the planet is to express ourselves freely. That's why we're here.

Steven Bartlett：你所说的这番话，恰好与我们之前探讨的内容紧密相连：在极度年幼的时候，我们就开始被灌输这样一种观念——我们所创造的事物仅仅是达成某种特定目的的工具，而这些目的无非是升职、加薪、积累财富、世俗成功和光鲜的职位头衔。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: What you're saying links to something we were talking about earlier, which is at a very young age, we're taught that the things we make are a means to a particular end, and that end is promotions, pay rises, capital, success, job titles.

Rick Rubin：没错。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yes.

Steven Bartlett：在具体的创作过程中，你的意思是说，去讨论我们将如何衡量成功、并试图围绕这种量化指标进行优化，其实永远都不具有建设性吗？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: In the creative process, are you saying that it's never productive to talk about how we'll measure success and to try and optimize for that measurement in the creative process?

Rick Rubin：我个人倾向于在创作时不去考虑这些东西。我想不出在任何创作场景下我们需要去依赖数据。

我认为创作更多关乎那种内在的纯粹感受，以及当你把它播放给某个人听、与某个人分享时所产生的情感共鸣——看它是否能够瞬间点亮一个人的神采。当某个人真的与一件作品产生深度共鸣时，你是能清晰感受到的。

有时候，冰冷的数据无法展现这一点，数字根本无法映射这种心领神会的体验。你知道，从商业指标上看，可能有两件产品在市场表现上不分伯仲，然而其中一件能彻底点燃你的激情与灵感，而另一件给人的感觉仅仅是“好吧，它确实勉强完成了任务”。这二者是完全不可同日而语的。

从长远来看，真正能够点亮你灵魂的那件作品，必然会对你产生更大的价值，你也更愿意在漫长的时间长河中持续为之付出和守候。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I I would I tend not to think about those things. I can't think of an example where we would want to use data.

I think it's it's much more about this internal feeling and then the feeling we have when you play it for someone, when you share it with someone, whether it lights someone up. You can tell when someone really resonates with something.

Sometimes the numbers don't show the numbers can't reflect that. You know, you can have two things that are kind of on equal footing commercially, yet one of them lights you up and the other one is, well, this gets the job done. They're different.

And longterm, chances are the one that lights you up is going to have more value to you and you'll be more willing to support that thing over time.

摆脱自我怀疑与比较的陷阱

Steven Bartlett：在你的著作《创造力的修行：一种存在的方式》（The Creative Act: A Way of Being）中，你专门剖析了那些阻碍创造力的因素——那些不利于创作的思想和习惯。其中一个就是：“深信自己还不够优秀。”

Original English

Steven Bartlett: In your book here, um, The Creative Act: A Way of Being, you talk about the things that block creativity, thoughts and habits not conducive to the work. Believing you're not good enough.

Rick Rubin：这是一个非常巨大的心理阻碍。在人生的某个时刻，比如当你还是个孩子正在唱歌时，有人随口说了一句：“哎呀，你根本不会唱歌”，这句话就此深深扎根在你心底，让你认定“唱歌是我永远都无法做到的事”；或者有人对你说“你是个糟糕的厨子”，或者“你不够聪明”。很多时候，我们与生俱来的天赋就这样被扼杀和窒息了。

我们每个人天生都拥有创造力。我们或许没有主动选择踏上一条创作之路，也没有去做出具有创造性的选择，但责任在于我们自己。我们可以做到，只是我们被塞满了错误的负面信息。我们从小被告知自己不够优秀，我们信以为真，但这根本不是事实。

当人们在日常生活中说：“你看，Rick 是个搞创意的人，而 Steven 不是搞创意的”，他们真正想表达的究竟是什么？在我们的文化语境中，成为一个“搞创意的人”究竟意味着什么？我们在概念的理解上出现了怎样的偏差？你能理解我的意思吗？因为大家习惯把人归类：“这个人搞创意，他做点艺术、搞搞音乐；那个人不搞创意，整天做 Excel 表格……”

Original English

Rick Rubin: That's a big one. Someone at some point in time you were singing and someone's like, "Ah, you can't sing" when you're a kid, and that gets lodged in you. Singing is not something I can ever do, or you're a terrible cook, or you're not smart enough. So often the gifts that we have are stifled and smothered.

We're all creative. We may not choose to embark on a creative make creative choices, but that's it's on us. We can we just have bad information. We we we're told we're not good enough and we believe it, and it's not true. When people say that, "Oh, you know, Rick is a creative and Steven is say they say Steven is not a creative." What are they actually saying? What is that like in our culture to be a creative? How like we misunderstanding the definition. You see what I'm saying? Cuz we do we go creative, uh they do a little bit of art and music stuff; creative, not creative, Excel documents, creative.

Steven Bartlett：我们在做这种划分时，到底是在表达什么？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: What are we actually saying there?

Rick Rubin：我认为这只是人们试图简化复杂世界的一种思维方式。世界本身是极其错综复杂的。我相信我们当中的每一个人都可以做任何事情。我并不是说我们所有人都能把每件事做到极致、比世界上任何其他人都做得好——这里面很大一部分问题源于“竞争”的概念。

就好比如果我想去打棒球，我知道通过练习我可以提升自己的棒球技术。这并不意味着我认为自己能厉害到去纽约大都会队（Mets）打职业比赛，但我确信我可以不断进步，我也知道我可以去训练任何我感兴趣的事物，并在此领域取得巨大长进。

然而现实是，一旦我们发现自己无法达到进入大都会队打球的顶级水平，我们就会觉得这项运动不适合自己，进而彻底将它关闭在生命之外。我们这样做根本是在亏待自己，导致我们无法成为最好的自己，也无法真正发挥出作为人类的全部潜能。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I think it's a way to um try to simplify the world. The world is complicated and I think all of us can do anything. I I'm not saying all of us could do everything to the best of our ability better than anyone else. That's a big part of it is the idea of competition.

Like if I want to play baseball, I know that I could get better at baseball. That doesn't mean I think I could get good enough to play on the Mets, but I know I could get better and I know I could train anything that I'm interested in and get much better at it.

And as soon as we're not capable of playing for the Mets, we think it's not for us and we shut that off. We're not doing a service to ourselves. We're not becoming our best selves or really uh living up to our full potential as humans.

Steven Bartlett：通过相互比较来衡量事物的价值，似乎是人类天然的本能。就像我们去餐厅点餐时一样：菜单上有三档价位的牛排，最贵的那份可能有点过于浮夸和奢侈，最便宜的那份可能品质不太好，那我干脆选中间那一档。这看似是人类为了偷懒而采取的一种认知捷径，但我们却把它套用在了审视自我上——我们总觉得，只有当我对比你的作品，发现我的排名更高、外观更漂亮或者更赚钱时，我的作品才是优秀的。

你是在建议我们彻底从这种相互比较的思维模式中跳脱出来吗？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Feels like a natural human instinct to compare in order to understand the value of anything. I feel like we do it when we go to restaurants. There's three steaks. The expensive one's probably a bit bougie. The cheap one's probably not good. I'll go for the middle one. Seems like a a way that humans take shortcuts, but we do it with ourselves as well. So, I know that the thing I've made is good as if I look over at your thing and mine is ranked better or mine is prettier or richer. Are you saying for us to get out of the business of comparison?

艺术无法横向比较与“懒惰的工作狂”

Rick Rubin：是的。因为在我看来，这永远是把苹果和橙子放在一起乱比，毫无意义。如果你去问迈克尔·杰克逊（Michael Jackson）和王子（Prince）谁更优秀？我会说，迈克尔·杰克逊在做迈克尔·杰克逊这件事上要出色得多，而王子在做王子这件事上也极其卓越。但你根本无法横向对比他们两人各自所做的事情，因为他们呈现的是两种完全截然不同的艺术表达。

就像你之前提到的坎耶·韦斯特（Kanye West）和德雷克（Drake），他们之间没有任何竞争可言。他们做的是两种彻底不同的东西。你从他们每一个人身上所获得的艺术感受是完全不一样的。我绝不会期望从德雷克那里得到坎耶给我的体验，也绝对不想从坎耶那里索取德雷克给我的感受。这种对比毫无意义。

至于谁更具创造力，那完全取决于每个人内心的主观感受。艺术家之间的横向竞争在根本上就是一个极其荒谬的概念。这又绕回到了老问题：就好像在争论谁写下的日记更高级一样，这太荒唐了。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yes. Because I'm saying it's it's always apples and oranges. If you if if you were to ask who's better, Michael Jackson or Prince, I would say Michael Jackson's much better at being Michael Jackson. And Prince is probably better at being Prince. But you can't compare what the two of them do. They do two different things.

Before you mentioned Kanye West and and Drake, there's no competition there. They do two totally different things. What you get from each of them is totally different. I wouldn't want from Drake what I get from Kanye. And I certainly wouldn't want from Kanye what I get from Drake. It's It's pointless.

Is either more creative? That's for you to decide. That's for you to decide. The whole idea of competition between artists is it's such a weird thing. Again, it's back to who has a better diary entry. It's ridiculous.

Steven Bartlett：书中列举的另一个阻碍创作的因素是：“总觉得自己没有足够的精力去承担这项工作。”这些心态都非常不利于创作。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Feeling you don't have the energy it takes. These are all things that are not conducive to the work.

Rick Rubin：是的，我自己有时也会产生这种感受——总觉得自己撑不住、负担太重了。我昨天刚从伦敦飞到这里，身体很累，还有点时差。但我依然会依约出现并与你对话。也许表现不会很好，也许躺下来放松、好好恢复状态才是更明智的选择。但我依然选择坐在这里，我准时到场了。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah, I have that one sometimes. Feeling like I'm not up to it too much. I flew here yesterday from London. I'm tired. I'm a little jetlagged. I'm going to show up and talk to you. Maybe it won't be good. Maybe it's better to just lay down, relax, and recover. But I'm here. I show up.

Steven Bartlett：你曾把自己形容为一个“懒惰的工作狂”（lazy workaholic）。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: You've referred to yourself as a um a lazy workaholic.

Rick Rubin：是的，确实如此。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yes, it's true.

Steven Bartlett：什么是“懒惰的工作狂”？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: What is a lazy workaholic?

Rick Rubin：通常在大多数日子里，我宁愿一件计划日程表上的事情都不去做。但我还是会制定好日程安排，按时到场，并把所有必须完成的事情一丝不苟地做好。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Often, most days, I would rather not do any of the things that I'm scheduled to do. And I schedule the things and I show up and I do what needs to get done.

Steven Bartlett：在创意工作中，你是否曾有过妥协将就的时候？比如唱片公司规定的最终交付截止日期（deadline）已经迫在眉睫，而作品尚未完全达到理想状态，你会说“行吧，我们就在这里收尾并提交”吗？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Do you ever settle with creative work? The the deadline for the record label is approaching. The work is not yet done. We'll finish here and we'll send it.

Rick Rubin：我通常不会这么做。在作品真正完成之前，我一般不会在有严格截止日期的压力下工作。我们会首次设定明确时间节点的唯一时刻……

Original English

Rick Rubin: I tend not to do that. I tend not to work on deadlines until we're past the point. The first time we would ever establish...

创作过程中的时间截点与探索空间

Rick Rubin：只有当我们确信已经破解了关键要点、明确知道它将呈现为什么形态并能清晰预见它时，才会设定截止日期。此时可能还没有完全完工，但我们对它是什么、将如何运转已经有了非常清晰的认知，进度大约达到了90%。到了这一步，我们可能会针对最后10%的收尾工作设定一个截止时间。而那最后的10%，通常并不会对整个项目的成败起到决定性作用。

Original English

Rick Rubin: A deadline is when we already know we've cracked the code. We know what this is going to be. We can see it. It may not be completely finished, but we have a good idea of what it is and how it's going to work, and it's maybe 90% done. Then we might set a deadline for that last 10% of finishing it. And that last 10% probably won't make or break the project.

Host: 当你践行刚才所描述的创作流程——即核心标准在于“我自己是否喜欢？我们自己是否喜欢？”——你必须接受的一点是：在某些时刻，你创造出来的东西会是绝对具有传奇色彩且独树一帜的，完全不同于现存的任何事物。但与此同时，你所承担的风险在于，你也可能会做出其他人根本不买账的东西。

Original English

Host: Part of what you accept when you pursue the creative process you've described—where it's really about like "Do I like it? Do we like it?"—is you will create things that are at some points just absolute legendary and unlike anything else that exists. They are not the same. But part of the risk you take is you'll make things that other people just don't like.

Rick Rubin：确实如此。在这个过程中仅仅就是：你产生了一个想法，然后动手去构建它。在这个过程中你可能会意识到，最初的想法或许并不是最好的，于是方向发生了偏转——原本打算做一座蓝色的雕塑，最后却变成了一幅红色的画作。你在刚开始时根本无法预料到这一点，而是在整个实践过程中逐步发现的，这可能需要花费很长时间。

而在另一些时候，你心想：“我要做这个蓝色的物件。”结果只花了5分钟就做好了，成品就摆在那里，大功告成。这种事情是你无法事先精确计算的，你无法预判接下来会发生什么。但如果我不在这里做这个实验，我就永远不可能触及到那个更好的结果。这种情况一直在发生。有时你做出了一个东西，它沉寂了整整20年都无人问津，直到后来它变成了亚马逊那样的体量。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah. That's all there is in this process is you have an idea, you build it. You may learn along the way that the idea was maybe not the best idea and it turns left and you make—I'm starting out to make this blue statue and it turns out to be this red painting. You don't know that going in, but you discover it through the process, and it could take a long time. Other times, you'll think, "I'm going to make this blue thing." You make it in 5 minutes. There it is. Done. And we can't—you can't calculate that. You can't anticipate what's going to happen. But if I wasn't doing this experiment here, I would never get to the better thing. It happens all the time. And then sometimes you build it and it doesn't work for 20 years and then it becomes Amazon.

时间跨度对生活与创作的塑造

Host: 我最近经常思考时间跨度（time horizons）是如何改变一个人所做的工作的，这也正是我从你刚才的话中所体会到的。因为在刚才那个蓝色雕塑的例子里，如果你身处严格的时间限制之下，你就根本没有空间去进行你所描述的那种探索。实际上在所有人的生活中，我认为我们都在施加时间跨度的设定。我们会说：我职业生涯的这个阶段设定为5年，在这5年里我想要达成这些目标。你如何看待为个人生活设定时间跨度，以及设定创作上的时间跨度？

Original English

Host: I've thought a lot recently about how time horizons change the work that you do. And that's kind of also what I'm hearing there because in that example you gave of this blue statue thing. If you were under a time horizon, you wouldn't have the space for that exploration you've described. And actually in all of our lives, we—I think apply time horizons. We say this part of my career is going to be 5 years, and in in those 5 years I want to achieve these things. How do you think about setting personal time horizons but also like creative time horizons?

Rick Rubin：我在书里谈到的一点是，创造力包含不同的阶段。第一个阶段是种子阶段（seed phase），接着是实验阶段（experimentation phase），然后是打磨构建阶段（crafting phase），最后是收尾完成与编辑阶段（finishing completion editorial phase）。在整个流程的前期，你根本无法设定时间表。

在种子阶段，你必须尝试大量的可能性，这需要耗费必要的时间，急不得。在实验阶段，你需要将那些种子拿出来，观察它们想要演化成什么形态，这同样需要时间。到了第三个阶段即打磨阶段，依然需要耗费时间，因为此时你已经拥有了种子，看到了它想成为的样子，并且给予了它充分孕育发酵的时间，看清了它是如何生长的；现在进入打磨阶段，你开始对其进行修剪与塑形，将其雕琢成你所预见的模样。

所有这些都必须在产生任何时间表概念之前完成，因为在那之前，你根本不知道自己到底在做什么东西，它还只是一个梦。但一旦你走完了这三个阶段，眼前已经有了实体雏形，它或许尚未完全收尾，但你已经看清了它的全貌。你看到了它，而且它非常出色，让你感到兴奋不已。

Original English

Rick Rubin: One of the things I talk about in the book is that there are different phases of creativity. The first phase is the seed phase, then is the experimentation phase, then is the crafting phase and then is the finishing completion editorial phase. And you can't set a timeline early in that process. In the seed phase, you have to try a lot of things and it's going to take as long as it takes. And in the experimental phase, which is where you're taking the seeds and seeing what they want to be, that takes time. And then in the crafting phase, the third phase still takes time because now you have the seed, you see what it wants to be. You've given it time to just state. You see how it grows. And now in the crafting phase, the third phase, you start trimming it and making it the way you see it. All of that has to happen before there's any idea of a timeline because until that time, you don't really know what you're making. It's a dream. But once you've gotten gone through these three phases and you have this thing in front of you, it may not be finished, but you see what it is. You see it and it's good. You're excited about it.

以艺术家的姿态面对生活

Rick Rubin：当我评价一件作品“很好”时，我的意思是对我而言它是好的。仅此而已，标准到此为止。只要它符合我的标准，我发自内心地认可它，以至于我愿意展示给你看；如果我足够喜欢它并愿意拿给你看，那么我也同样愿意把它展示给全世界的所有人。

Original English

Rick Rubin: When I say it's good, I mean it's good to me. That's it. That's where it ends. It's good to me. I like it enough that I'm willing to show it to you. If I like it enough that I'm willing to show it to you, I like it enough to show it to everybody.

Host: 许多人听到这些可能会当成创作建议，而我听到的却是一套人生建议。我从中听到了人生的种子阶段、人生的实验阶段以及打磨阶段。

Original English

Host: People will hear this as creative advice. I was hearing it as life advice. I was hearing the seed phase of my life, the experimentation phase of my life, the crafting phase.

Rick Rubin：它们本质上是一回事，完全相通。这也是为什么《创造力的修行》（The Creative Act）最初是围绕“如何创造事物”这一想法展开的，但通过撰写这本书我逐渐意识到，它其实关乎“如何在这个世界上生活与自处”。你要么选择让生活为这些美好的发生敞开空间，要么选择不敞开。如果你以一种允许这些事物自然发生的方式去生活，那么你就是一名艺术家。

无论你是一名商人、厨师，还是一位母亲，你都是在以艺术家的姿态过着属于你的人生。你将这种状态融入自身并由内而外地展现出来。我想说这就是你的本质，哪怕它不代表你的全部，也代表了你在那一当下的真实自我。我向你展示这件美好的事物，是在折射我今天看待这个世界的方式。到了明天，我眼中的世界或许又截然不同，这完全没有问题。但在我与你分享的这一刻，这就是它在当前所能达到的最完美状态。

Original English

Rick Rubin: It's the same. It's all the same. And that's why the the creative act started with this idea of how to make things. And it turned—what I realized through writing the book is it's how to be in the world that that you you either live your life to allow these things to happen or you don't. And if you live your life to allow these things to happen, then you're an artist. Even if you're a businessman or if you're a chef or if you're a mother, you're living your life as an artist. You're embodying it and it's coming from you and it's—I want to say it's who you are. It's not who you are, but it's who you are in that moment. I'm showing you this beautiful thing as a reflection of how I see the world today. Now, tomorrow I may see the world differently. That's fine. But when I share it with you, this is the best it can be in this moment.

风险、现实约束与纯粹动机

Host: 我之所以提出关于“有时世界可能不喜欢你所交付的作品”这个问题……

Original English

Host: The reason I was asking this question about the um you know sometimes they the world might not like what you give it.

Rick Rubin：是的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah.

Host: 是因为“失败”这个词。你在书里也谈到了失败，以及该如何去应对失败。我想很多人之所以不敢冒这个风险去倾听自己内心的声音、去追求自己真正热爱的事物，就是因为“风险”这两个字。伴随风险而来的担忧是：我可能会损失金钱，我可能会失去朋友；如果我辞掉这份工作去追求拉小提琴的爱好，跑到秘鲁的高山上去拉琴，我会失去朋友，大家会觉得我是个怪人，所以我宁可选择顺从主流。

Original English

Host: Is because because of this word failure which you talk about in the book as well and how to deal with failure. I guess a lot of the reason why people won't take the risk to listen to themselves to what they love is because of the word risk. And with that comes I might lose money. I might lose friends. If I quit this job and pursue that violin hobby and go to Peru and violin up in Peru, my I'm going to lose friends. People are going to think I'm strange. So I I'll conform.

Rick Rubin：是的。而我的主张是：你必须去直面所有这些事情。失去那些朋友也无妨，去承担这个风险。但我并不是说只要你搬去秘鲁当个小提琴手就百分之百能成功；在做这些的同时，你可能需要找一份实际的工作来支付日常开销账单，这完全没有问题。

人们常常有一种误区，认为所有事情必须融为一体——我必须从事我梦想的事业，它必须同时是我的爱好、我的挚爱和我的职业。但你用来维持生计的手段并不非得与创作捆绑在一起。当我最初开始做音乐时，我本以为自己会去找一份常规工作，把做音乐当成一辈子的业余爱好。我会永远做下去，完全按照当时的方式去做；但我以为自己必须靠一份正经工作来养活自己，从而支撑我去做那些我真正想做的事情。

幸运的是，音乐后来自然而然地发展成了我的一番事业，而这并非出于我刻意的谋划。我起初根本不指望它能成为一种职业，甚至觉得那是个荒谬的想法，我做音乐纯粹是因为我热爱它。那时我一边上学，甚至可能打算去读法学院，然后找一份正经的日常全职工作，把音乐作为我一生的至爱。我原本的打算就是这样。幸运的是我最终不必走那条路，但即使走了那条路也是顺理成章的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah. And I'm saying you have to do all of those things. Lose the friends. Take the risk. Now, I'm not I'm and and I'm also saying I'm not saying that for that 100% if you go move to Peru and be the violinist, it's going to work. And you may want to have an actual job to pay the bills while you're doing that. That's okay. The idea that it all has to be one thing. I have to do my dream thing and it has to be my prof—it has to be my hobby, my love, my profession. The way I support myself doesn't have to be. When I started making music, I thought I was going to have a real job and I'd make music and it would be my hobby. I would do it forever. I would do exactly what I was doing. But I thought I would have a real job to support myself to do these things that I want to do. Luckily, I was able to—it it turned into its own thing by nothing that I did. Had no expectation that that it could be a profession. I had no—it couldn't be. It was a ridiculous idea. I did it because I loved it. And while I was going to school and while I was probably going to go go to law school and I would have a regular job, day job, and I would do music as the love of my life. That's what I would do. Luckily, I didn't have to do that. But that's what would have happened.

不将生存压力转嫁给艺术

Host: 许多处于那个年龄段的年轻人被告知要去追随自己的热情，去寻找人生的目标。这听起来似乎也是这个意思。因为“热情”（passion）是一个单数词，而“寻找”（find）意味着必须主动外出追寻某个特定的事物。现在很多收听节目的听众可能会说：“我一直被告诫要去找到自己的热情所在。”当他们看着自己在工厂或任何地方所从事的工作时，他们会想：“好吧，虽然现在这份工作不是我真正想要的，但我以后总有一天会达成目标的。”

Original English

Host: A lot of people at that age are told to follow their passion, find their purpose, which sounds like this, you know, cuz the word passion is a singular word. And also the word find means I have to go go out there and actively pursue the thing. A lot of people listening now would would say, you know, I've been told to find my passion and when they look at their job in the factory or wherever it might be, whatever they're doing, they go, okay, this isn't it yet, but I will get there at some point later.

Rick Rubin：是的。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Yeah.

Host: “到时候我就会纵身一跃。”所以你的意思是，并不一定要孤注一掷地纵身跳跃？

Original English

Host: And I will jump. So you're saying don't jump necessarily.

Rick Rubin：生活中确实存在必须面对的现实，我并不是让你无视必须交房租的事实，或者说哪怕赖在父母家里直到成功也无所谓——那样也可以。我的意思是，生活中的现实问题必须得到妥善处理，切忌把这些现实生存的压力转嫁到你的艺术创作上。因为一旦转嫁了压力，你就无法做出最纯粹、最合理的创作决定；你会迫于眼下必须应对的现实问题而做出短视的妥协，从而损害你所从事的创作的长期价值，侵蚀你真正付诸心血的工作。

Original English

Rick Rubin: There are realities of life that you have to deal with. I'm not saying ignore the fact that you get you have to pay rent or or say I'm happy living in my parents house until until it works. That's fine. I'm saying there are realities of life that have to be dealt with. Don't put the pressure of of that on your art because then you won't make the best decisions. You'll make short-term decisions based on real world issues that you have to deal with in that moment that won't support the long-term benefit of the work you're doing, the real work you're doing.

合作中的角色扮演与引导

Host: 我确信你也经历过那些时刻，试图去说服一位艺术家遵从他们自己的内心和他们所……

Original English

Host: I'm sure you've sat there in those moments where you're trying to persuade an artist to follow themselves and what they—

Rick Rubin：不，我从不试图去说服任何人接受任何事情。

Original English

Rick Rubin: No, I don't try to persuade anyone of anything.

Host: 噢，真的吗？好吧。

Original English

Host: Oh, really? Okay.

Rick Rubin：我不会使用“说服”（persuade）这个词。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I don't I wouldn't use that word. I wouldn't use the word persuade.

Host: 那你会用什么词呢？

Original English

Host: What word would you use?

Rick Rubin：我们会共同探讨。我会询问：“你的目标是什么？你期望达成什么样的成果？”然后在此基础上，我会为那一过程提供支持与协助。

Original English

Rick Rubin: We talk about it. I I ask what are your what do you hope to accomplish and then I'd support that process.

Host: 我们可以来做个角色扮演吗？我假装自己是一个想要录制一张专辑的艺术家。实际上我最近在学习当一名DJ，已经学了大概4年了。你会对我说些什么呢？假设我过来对你说：“Rick，我想做一张专辑。”

Original English

Host: Can we role play? I pretend I'm an artist who wants to write an album. So, okay. So, I am actually learning to DJ. I've been learning for like 4 years. What would you say to me? So, I come say, "Rick, I want to make an album."

Rick Rubin：你会……

Original English

Rick Rubin: What would—

倾听与扫清障碍

采访者：你会问我的第一个问题会是什么？

Original English

Interviewer: ...the first questions be to me?

制作人：放你做过的所有作品里，放你最喜欢的作品给我听。让我们来聊聊它。我会听，然后我们一起讨论。

Original English

Producer: Play me the—of all the things you've made, play me your favorites. Let's talk about it. I would listen to it and then we would talk about it.

采访者：所以，比如我放了一首浩室音乐（House music）给你听。我放了之后说：“这是我最喜欢的作品。”

Original English

Interviewer: So, I play you, let's say it's a House song. I play it. I said, "This is my favorite."

制作人：我会如实告诉你我对它的所有真实想法。坦诚地说。

Original English

Producer: I would tell you whatever I think about it. Honestly.

采访者：非常坦诚，哪怕你觉得它糟糕透顶？

Original English

Interviewer: Honestly, even if you thought it was terrible.

制作人：是的。我大概不会用令人不悦的方式去表达，但我可能会围绕它提出更多的问题。去深入探寻，比如，向我解释一下你到底喜欢它的哪一点？你是怎么做出这个效果的？你当时使用了什么设备？制作它的过程充满乐趣吗？那是一段愉快的体验吗？你有没有把它放给其他人听过？感觉怎么样？那种体验如何？你在创作它时内心是什么感受？当你放给朋友听时又是什么感受？你曾在俱乐部里播放过它吗？现场反响如何？我会认真倾听你所说的一切，然后我们再就此展开交流。

很多时候，艺术家来找我，他们会一清二楚地告诉我他们想做什么，但紧接着又说：“我完全不知道自己想做什么，你能帮帮我吗？”这就像他们听不见自己的声音一样。我们所有人之间都存在这样一道屏障。这其实有点像心理咨询的机制：当你去看心理咨询师时，你可能一直在倾诉，咨询师也许一言不发，但正是在倾诉的过程中，你开始理解自己坚信的是什么、思考的是什么，以及生活中究竟在发生什么。有时仅仅因为有人在认真倾听，你就会豁然开朗。

大多数人并不习惯被倾听——被真正地、深入地倾听。通常当你对别人说话时，对方往往在心里盘算着自己接下来要讲什么，他们并没有真正听到你所说的话。而我发现，如果你全神贯注地在场，真正去倾听对方在表达什么，他们其实会把自己想做的事全盘托出。而我的工作，仅仅是帮助他们扫清他们自己在前进道路上设置的阻碍。

Original English

Producer: Yeah. I probably wouldn't say it in an unpleasant way, but I would probably ask more questions around it. Explain to me what it is that you like about it. How did you get to that? What equipment did you use? Was it fun making it? Was it a good experience? Have you played it for other people? What's it like? What's the experience like? What's the feeling you get when you're making it? What's the feeling you get when you play it for a friend? Have you ever played it in a club? What's that like? I would listen to what you say and then we would talk about it.

Often an artist will come to me, they'll tell me exactly what they want to do and then say, "I have no idea what I want to do. Can you help me?" It's like they can't hear themselves. There's a—we all have this barrier between—it's a little bit how therapy works. If you go to see a therapist, you might talk. They might not say anything, but through the talking you start understanding what you believe, what you think, what's going on in your life. You might have epiphanies just because someone's listening.

Most people are not used to being heard, really heard, deeply heard. Often you will say something to someone and they're thinking about what they're going to say next. They're not really hearing what you're saying. And I find if you really are there and listen to what someone's saying, they tell you what they want to do and then my job is to just help remove the roadblocks that they're putting in the way of themselves to get there.

采访者：当他们明确说出了自己想做什么，却又反过来问你他们该怎么做时——

Original English

Interviewer: And when they say what they want to do but then ask you what they should do—

制作人：是的。

Original English

Producer: Yeah.

采访者：这是一种缺乏自我信任的表现吗？这究竟是什么原因？

Original English

Interviewer: Is that a lack of sort of self-trust? What is that?

自我信任与“披萨法则”

制作人：是的，没错。但这种自我信任的缺失，本就是整个世界的常态。这是我们被灌输、被驯化的结果。我们大多数人都不相信自己。这其实是一条微妙的界限，因为有极少数人看起来极度自信，你可能会觉得：“噢，他们是自大狂。”但通常当一个人表现得极度狂妄自大时，往往是为了掩盖内心深处的某些不安全感。那就像一副面具。

所以，对待一个缺乏安全感的人，和对待一个过度自信的人，往往是同一枚硬币的两面。而我们真正在寻找的，是处于现实中的中间地带：不是你对自己所恐惧的事物，也不是其他人会怎么看你，而是你内心深处对这件事的真实态度到底是什么。

我常常把这比作品尝食物。比如我递给你两块披萨并说：“尝尝这两块披萨，告诉我你更喜欢哪一块。”这不是什么难回答的问题。你大概会说：“比起那块，我明显更喜欢这一块。”当我问你哪块更好吃时，你绝不会回答：“我觉得那个人可能会更喜欢这块披萨。”你只会说：“对我来说，这块更好吃。”

我们的目标就是回到这种境界——“对我而言，这块更好吃”。就是这么简单。我们总是不敢相信事情本可以如此纯粹简单，但它事实上的确如此简单。

Original English

Producer: Yeah. Yes. But that lack of self-trust is what is the condition of the world. That's how we're indoctrinated. Most people don't trust themselves. And it's a fine line because there are the few people who might seem to really trust themselves and you think, "Oh, they're egomaniacs." Usually when someone really comes off as wildly egotistical, it's usually hiding some insecurities really. It's like a mask.

So that's dealing with someone who's insecure and dealing with someone who's overconfident. It's often two sides of the same coin. And we're really looking for this middle place of reality. It's like not what you're afraid of about yourself, or what other people are going to think, but where are you really on this?

I often describe it as tasting food. Like if I gave you two pieces of pizza and say, "Taste the two pieces of pizza. Tell me which one you like better." It's not a hard question. You'll probably say, "I like this one much better than this." And when I ask you which tastes better, you wouldn't say, "Well, I think this guy's going to like this pizza better." You would say, "This one tastes better to me." That's the goal is getting to that, "This one tastes better to me." Simple. We don't believe that it can be as simple as it is. And it is simple.

采访者：如果你要在某位艺术家身上下注，押注他能成为与你合作过的那些伟大艺术家之一，是因为他们具备某些特质、某种视角或谦逊品质，而这些与你所见过的能产出卓越作品的因素相吻合。那么这些相似的特质具体是什么呢？作为一名经常投资创业者的人，我想知道这两者之间是否存在共通之处，因为听起来有许多相通的地方。

Original English

Interviewer: If you were to bet on an artist being one of these great artists that you've worked with because they have a set of characteristics or a perspective or a humility that is conducive with what you've seen results in exceptional work, what are those similar attributes? As someone that does a lot of investing in entrepreneurs, I was wondering if there were similarities even there, cuz some of it sounded the same.

看人而非看产品：The Strokes 的故事

制作人：我确信是存在相通之处的。我曾问过一位做风险投资的朋友，当他在挑选投资标的时，看重的是商业模式本身，还是创始人？他回答说，永远是创始人，做什么业务其实无所谓。

Original English

Producer: I'm sure there are. I asked a friend of mine who is a venture capital guy about when he's picking things to invest in, is it the premise of the business or is it the founder? And he said, it's always the founder, doesn't matter what the business is.

采访者：他说只要认准了这个人——

Original English

Interviewer: He said, if I believe in the person—

制作人：就会全力支持他们。

Original English

Producer: I'll back them.

采访者：嗯。

Original English

Interviewer: Mhm.

制作人：我想对待艺术家也是完全一样的道理。关键在于艺术家这个人，而不是“他们写出了一首好歌”。一首好歌更像是一个优秀的产品，也许他们还能再做出来一次。但如果你能看到他们理解音乐的方式，看到他们在创作过程中的创新能力，那就是一个非常积极的信号。

给你讲个故事吧。关于 The Strokes 乐队。

Original English

Producer: And I suppose it would be the same with artists. It's the artists, not like, "Well, they wrote a great song." The great song is more like the great product. It's like, maybe they could do that again. But if you see that the way that they see music, the way that they innovate in the things that they make, that's a good sign. Tell you a story: the band The Strokes.

制作人：我给他们制作了最新的一张专辑，大概一个月前刚刚发行。而在大约15年前，他们就曾邀请我为他们制作专辑。当时我听了那些 Demo（小样），但我没听出感觉来。我并不是说那些作品不可能变好，我只是找不到切入的路径。对我来说，感觉就像：“我不觉得我应该接这个案子，我没有共鸣。”后来大约在12年前，他们又一次邀请我，我又听了小样，但我依然没感觉。我就是看不到该怎么去打磨它，于是我又拒绝了。

Original English

Producer: I produced their new album, just came out maybe a month ago, and they had asked me to produce an album for them maybe, I want to say 15 years ago, and I listened to the demos, and I didn't hear it. I'm not saying it couldn't be good. I just didn't see the way in. For me, it's like, I don't think I should do this. I don't feel it. And then they asked me another time maybe 12 years ago and I listened to the demos and I didn't hear it. I just don't see how to do it. So I passed.

采访者：“看不到该怎么做”——能具体描述一下这是什么意思吗？

Original English

Interviewer: I didn't see how to do it. Give me some color on what that means.

制作人：是的。这就好比，当我听到某些旋律时，我能清晰地看到一条路径，能捕捉到某种质感。它不一定非要已经很完善，但我能从中听到某种东西，让我明白这是什么，并且让我产生渴望，想看看它最终能演变成什么样。

Original English

Producer: Yeah. It's like when I hear something and I can see a path. I can hear a thing. It doesn't have to be good, but I could hear something in it of like, I understand what this is and I want to see what this can become.

制作人：前两次我完全没有这种感觉。直到我们合作的第一张专辑（大约6年前发行的那张），他们再次把小样发给我。那可能是他们发过来的所有小样里制作最粗糙的一批，但我却能听出它们可以变成什么样。其中一个小样甚至只是一段20秒的语音备忘录。而在过去，他们发来的都是经过精心编排制作的 Demo，标榜着“这是我们的全新力作”。但就在那段20秒的语音备忘录里，我听着听着就心想：我真的太想听到整首歌了。无论这颗创意的种子究竟是什么，它都注定会是一首杰作，它具备成为卓越作品的潜能。

Original English

Producer: I didn't feel that the first two times. And then on the first album we worked on together, which came out about 6 years ago, they sent me demos again. They were probably the worst demos they ever sent, but I could hear what it could be. And one of the demos might be a 20-second voice note. Whereas in the past there were these produced demos of like, "Here's our great new songs." But in this 20-second voice note I could listen and think, "I really want to hear this whole song. Whatever this is, this seed of an idea, this is going to be good. Has the potential to really be good."

采访者：你怎么知道它会好呢？

Original English

Interviewer: How do you know?

直觉、无常与不设目标的人生

制作人：去感受它。这世上根本没有所谓的“理性预知”，那是一种全然的直觉感知（knowingness）。就像我心里自然通晓一样，我无法向你逻辑性地解释它，那是一种纯粹的内在体验。其中一个标志是，你会本能地想要身体前倾，而不是往后靠；你会不由自主地想要倒回去重听一遍，而不是跳到下一首。它激发了好奇心，激发了浓厚的兴趣，让你渴望了解更多。

Original English

Producer: Feel it. There is no knowing. It's a knowingness. It's like I have a knowingness. I couldn't explain it to you. It's an internal experience. One of the things is, again, you tend to want to lean forward, not lean back. You tend to want to listen again, not skip to the next one. It generates curiosity. It generates interest and I want to know more.

采访者：你在自己的生活中会设立目标吗？

Original English

Interviewer: Do you set goals in your own life?

制作人：从来不设。

Original English

Producer: None.

采访者：你给我的印象是一个完全能坦然接纳不确定性的人。而在现实世界中，大多数人都需要确定感才能产生前行的动力，或者朝着某个方向采取行动。

Original English

Interviewer: You strike me as someone who is okay with uncertainty in a world where most people need certainty in order to take forward motion or take an action in a direction.

制作人：我知道不确定性是真实不虚的。我曾在马里布拥有一栋房子，那是我的主宅，里面存放着我所有的个人物品。8年前，那栋房子在一场大火中彻底化为灰烬。当那一切发生时，你很快就会明白：我原本以为自己会永远住在那里。当灾难降临时，我真切地意识到：无常才是生命的真相。

我曾以为我会一直在那里住下去，而现在它已经荡然无存了。那里只剩下一片空地，一块光秃秃的泥土地。所以，我不认为我们能够预知任何事情。我们只是顺着生命前行，根据当下所掌握的信息做出抉择，然后静待生命的自然展开。

Original English

Producer: I know uncertainty is real. I had a house in Malibu that was my main house with all of my possessions that 8 years ago burned in a fire. And when that happened, you very quickly see like, I thought I would live there forever. When that happened, I realized, hm, impermanence is real.

I thought I was going to live there forever. It's gone now. It's a dirt lot. So, I don't think that we can know anything. We just kind of move along and make decisions based on the information we have at the time and life unfolds.

采访者：我想可以用车载导航来打个比方。你早晨坐进车里，系好安全带，然后设置好车载导航说：“这是我的目的地，这是我想去的地方。”接着你发动车子开过去。也就是说，你为了实现那个目标付诸了行动。而你刚才所说的听起来更像是：你只是漫无目的地开车兜风。

Original English

Interviewer: I guess an analogy would be satnav in the car. You get in the car in the morning, you put your seat belt on, then you set your satnav and say, "This is my goal. This is where I want to go." And then you drive. So, you put action towards that goal. What you're saying sounds more like you just drive.

制作人：是的，一直如此。我过去经常那样做。真有意思你提到了这一点。我记得曾经有一段时间，我整天泡在录音室里高强度工作。工作时间极长，录音日程极其密集。有时候我一年要制作8张专辑，同时在好几个不同的项目之间来回切换。到了周末好不容易有一天空闲，我绝不制定任何计划，就是开着车在路上转，一边在车里听音乐一边兜风。我可能会开到某个地方，也可能只是漫无目的地开上几个小时来放松身心，那种感觉棒极了。

Original English

Producer: Yeah. All the time. Yeah. I used to do that. It's funny you bring that up, but there was a period of time when I worked a lot in the recording studio. A lot of hours, many sessions. At sometimes I made eight albums a year and I'd be working on different projects at the same time, and then I would have a day off on the weekend and I wouldn't make any plans, but I would just drive and just listen to music in the car and drive. And I might end up somewhere, but I might just drive for a couple of hours and relax, and it felt good.

采访者：但你可能会迷路，这就是风险所在。

Original English

Interviewer: You might get lost though, and that's the risk.

制作人：那完全没关系。

Original English

Producer: That's okay.

采访者：这就是为什么我们在生活中需要设立目标，为什么我们要制定五年规划、新年愿望。是的，我一直——

Original English

Interviewer: That's why we have goals in our life. That's why we make a 5-year plan, a New Year's resolution. Yeah, I've—

接纳未知与打破自我设限

Rick Rubin：我从来没有做过那些事情，甚至完全无法想象。这听起来像是一种可怕的局限。无论事情能有多好，我都无法想象那种状态。如果是那样，我对世界的理解就会比现实中发生的一切平庸得多，也无法经常看到“不可能”化为“可能”。我当然无法对此做出规划。如今，正因为这样的事情在我生命中发生过太多次，我已经坦然接受这就是现实，并且我正享受其中。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Never done any of those things. Couldn't imagine it. Seems like a terrible limitation. As good as it can be, I could never imagine that. I would have much more ordinary understanding of the world than what actually happens, than seeing the impossible become possible on a regular basis. I certainly couldn't plan for that. Now, because it's happened so much in my life, I accept that's how it is and I'm on the ride.

Steven Bartlett：所以你接纳了未知和不确定性。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: You accept the unknown and the uncertainty.

Rick Rubin：接纳未知。

Original English

Rick Rubin: The unknown.

Steven Bartlett：这是我们所有人都能做到的事情，还是说这是你如今取得如此巨大成功后才拥有的特权？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Is that something that we can all do or is that a privilege that you have now that you're so successful in that...

Rick Rubin：我们在太多方面限制了自己。我记得有一位非常成功的艺术家，当时正经历一段极其艰难的时期。我们在马里布的一家餐厅吃晚饭，我清晰地记得具体是哪家餐厅。我当时甚至觉得这个人可能会自杀。我能感受到他的能量是如此失衡、如此充满内在冲突，他承受着巨大的痛苦。

Original English

Rick Rubin: We limit ourselves in so many ways. I can remember a very successful artist who was going through a really hard time went out to dinner at this restaurant in Malibu. I remember specifically what it was. And I remember thinking this person might commit suicide. That's how I could feel their energy was so unbalanced and so conflicted and they were suffering.

Rick Rubin：我记得我对他说：“你知道吗，你其实不必继续做这些事。你已经做成了很多了不起的事情，取得了巨大的成功，你完全承担得起不再做这些的代价。任何时候只要你愿意，你都可以说：‘你知道吗？我要搬到世界的另一端，住在海滩边，吃好吃的食物，在水里嬉戏。’你没有义务继续去做那些给你的生活带来如此多冲突的事情。你已经做得足够多了。”

Original English

Rick Rubin: And I remember saying, you know, you don't have to keep doing this. You know, you've done a lot of great things. You've had great success. You can afford not to do this. And anytime you want to, you can say, you know what? I'm going to move to the other side of the world and live on the beach and just eat good food, play in the water. You're not obligated to continue doing what you're doing that's causing so much conflict in your life. You've done enough.

Rick Rubin：多年后我才知道，当时我都不知道他到底有没有听进去我说的话，因为他那时正处于非常痛苦的境地。但多年后，他对我说：“还记得你当时说的话吗？你说我可以离开，我可以选择退出。”他确实退出了。他退出的方式并非我当时想象的那样，我当时也不知道事情会那样发展，但他确实做出了改变。

Original English

Rick Rubin: And I found out years later, I didn't even know that the person really heard what I was saying cuz they were really in a hard place. But years later, that person said to me, "Remember what you said? You said I could check out." And he did. He didn't do it in the way that I imagined. I didn't know that that's what happened, but he did that.

需要终结的是处境，而非生命

Rick Rubin：我认识好几个自杀身亡的人，我还有一个好朋友曾自杀未遂。他们在那些时刻，往往都觉得是自己过去的种种选择将自己困住了，并且走投无路。当一个人产生自杀念头时，内心深处其实知道有某种东西需要被终结，但他误以为需要终结的是肉体生命。

Original English

Rick Rubin: I know several people who committed suicide and I have one friend, a good friend who unsuccessfully tried to commit suicide. All of them, in those moments, it's like they feel like the choices I've made have me boxed in and there's no way out. And when someone thinks that, the inclination to commit suicide is the person knows something needs to die and they think the body needs to die.

重置人生与打破困境

主持人：……这只是一套说辞，并不是真的。我们其实是自由的。对于那些想要按下“重置键”的人来说，朝着那个方向迈出的第一步是什么？有这样一个起点吗？

Original English

Host: ...just a story. It's not true. We are free. Is there a step one in that direction for people that want to hit the reset button? Is there a first step?

Rick Rubin：嗯，我会试着去审视生活中有哪些事情是你真正想要改变的，然后思考以怎样最干净利落的方式去做出改变——无论是辞去一份工作，还是离开一段亲密关系。你要知道，之所以会陷入这种局面，是因为我们最初带着良好的初衷许下了承诺；而当兑现承诺变得艰难时，我们就会开始感到被围困其中。

其实我认为做出承诺并坚守承诺是一件好事。但话虽如此，有时事情会发展到生死攸关的地步。这就像是说：我当初许下的这个承诺是个错误，或者情况发生了变化。比如，我的结婚对象变成了酗酒者，而和一个嗜酒如命的人一起生活极其痛苦，毫无幸福可言。这根本不是我们当初承诺的生活。我可以竭尽所能去帮助对方，但也许一切尝试都无济于事。在某个时刻，按下重置键是完全没问题的，你应该意识到：如果你继续被困在这样的处境里，对任何人都毫无益处。

我有很多朋友深处亲密关系中，也许感情早已急转直下，但他们依然耗在里面，因为双方都觉得“这就是我们当初选择并承诺的生活”。然而事实上，在一段关系里郁郁寡欢，你并没有在给对方任何恩惠或帮助。爱的本质是一条双向道；这就好比如果一个人爱着另一个人，而对方并不爱他——也就是所谓的单相思——其实严格来说那根本算不上真正的爱。因为爱的本质就像磁铁的正负极互相吸引，你不可能让磁铁只有一端在恋爱，这根本行不通。那只是一种关于“可能有多美好”的幻想，作为乐观主义者，你觉得它本可以很美好；但如果爱并不存在，那么分开对所有相关的人才是最好的。

我有时有些朋友会觉得：“我不想和对方分手，因为那会毁了他们。” 这并不是真的。如果一段关系已经无法维系，让彼此走向各自全新的美好生活，对每个人都是更好的选择。你可以这么做，这完全没问题。

我也能想象，有些人生活中存在着许许多多微小的失调与错位。单看任何一件，都不是什么天大的问题，但日积月累，他们的生活里充斥着上百件细小失调的事情，可能是各方面的。因此，他们的整个人生感觉从根本上脱了节，但实际上，可能只是因为他们在生活中的很多细微角落里一直在逃避坦诚与真实。

Original English

Rick Rubin: Well, I would try to see what are the things in your life that really you want to change and then think about what's the cleanest way to change them, leaving a job, leaving a relationship. You know, the reason these things happen is we make commitments with good intentions, and then when it's hard to keep the commitment is where it starts feeling boxed in. Now, I think commitments are a good thing both to make and to keep. That said, there can be a point where it becomes life and death. I have a lot of friends who are in relationships, and maybe the relationship has gone south, but they stay in it because both sides of the couple feel like, "This is the life we signed up for." And in reality, you're not doing any favors for the person being unhappy in a relationship. The nature of love is it's a two-way street. You can't have one side of the magnet in love. It doesn't work. And it's just a fantasy of what you know, it's a beautiful fantasy of what you think like as an optimist, it could be so beautiful, but if it's not there, it's best for everyone involved.

Sometime I have some friends who feel like, "Well, I don't want to break up with them because it'll destroy them." It's not true. It's better for everyone involved if it's not working to find the new beautiful life. You can do that. It's okay.

I imagine as well there's some people who have lots of small misalignments. None of them are huge issues on their own, but now they have a life full of a hundred small misaligned things. Could be anything. And so their life feels really fundamentally misaligned, but it's actually just maybe they struggled with honesty across many little areas of their life.

主持人：是的。也许那些问题还可以通过努力去改善，也许还能修复。但无论哪种情况，都不能放任其拖延下去，得去修复它们。但这往往意味着必须进行艰难的对话，Rick。

Rick Rubin：事情是这样的：我是家里的独生子，父母非常爱我、关心我，始终全力支持我。我去上学，把做音乐当成业余爱好，结果音乐事业自然而然地生根发芽，演变成了一门正规商业运作，那些年里我也取得了巨大的成功。然而到了33岁时，这种一帆风顺的人生第一次遭到了重创与质疑。

当时我和一家大型唱片公司有着业务合作。与我对接的那位高管可以说是整个音乐界数一数二的泰斗级人物，深得大家爱戴，为人极好，也是我的导师之一。但他后来在公司内部的政治斗争中被排挤出局，换上了一个我素不相识的新领导。那个人给我打电话说：“我看了你的——我看了我们的合同协议，”因为他直接接管了我之前与导师签下的协议。他说：“我很不喜欢这份协议。等你回加州的时候，我们得好好谈谈。” 仅此而已，整个通话就这么一句话。

可是对我而言，我完全不知所措。因为在此之前我顺风顺水了太久，生活中从不需要面对这种不确定性和动荡。我感觉脚下的地毯被瞬间抽走了，随后我开始出现惊恐发作，彻夜失眠，病得很重。

对于几乎任何其他人来说，同样的场景可能根本算不上什么大事，他们会说：“行，等我回去再解决，没问题”，然后继续过日子。但我当时完全没有应对这种挫折的能力，因为我的人生从未经历过类似的事情，我缺乏应对这种打击的心智肌肉。我感觉天塌地陷，完全不知道该怎么办，整个人彻底沉沦了下去。这种情况持续了好几年。即便后来问题解决了——我离开了那家公司，与新公司建立了合作关系，一切都重回正轨——但我感觉自己就像是在那一刻被扎破了的气球。我再也回不到从前的自己了。

不过，现在的我或许更加脚踏实地、扎根于现实之中了——听完我之前说的那些话，这听起来可能有点好笑。但我以前确实从未怀疑过任何事情，一切总是顺理成章地沿着同一个方向发展。那是我的信念第一次被打破，它彻底击垮了我，我花了数年时间才从深渊中爬出来。

但有一点我必须说，正是因为有了那段经历，面对现在合作的许多深受个人问题困扰的艺术家，我才能感同身受。在33岁之前，我可能无法像现在这样深刻体会他们的痛苦；而现在，我亲身经历过那种万丈深渊、底线尽失的感觉。可以说，它极大地加深了我对与我合作的艺术家的理解。

Original English

Rick Rubin: What happened was I had been an only child. My parents really loved me, really cared about me, always supportive. I went to school. I started making music as a hobby. It ended up taking on a life of its own and becoming a business and had great success for all those years. And then at 33 for the first time it got called into question.

I had a business relationship with a big company. The person who I was in relationship with was one of the probably the top person in the whole music industry. Well-loved, beautiful person, one of my mentors. He got politically forced out and a new person was put in who I didn't have a relationship with. And that person called me and said, "I read your I read our agreement"—because he had adopted the agreement that I had with my mentor. He's like, "I don't like it. When you come back to California, we should talk about it." That was it. That was the whole conversation.

Now, for me, I didn't know because I'd had so much success for so long. I never had to deal with any instability in my life in that way. I felt like the rug had been pulled out from under me, and I started having panic attacks and couldn't sleep and got really sick. And again, for almost anyone else, the exact same scenario would have been, "Okay, we'll deal with it when I get there, no problem," and move on. But I didn't have the ability to deal with it because I never had to deal with anything like that. I didn't have the musculature to deal with that issue, and it felt like the rug was pulled out. I didn't know what to do and sunk, and then it ended up going on for years.

Even after the problem was solved—I ended up leaving the company, starting a relationship with a new company, everything was fine—but it was like I was a balloon that got burst in that moment. And I'm still not the same person as I was before. Now maybe now I'm more rooted in reality, which probably sounds funny after a lot of the things I've been saying before, that I would say I never questioned anything. It was just like everything was always rolling in this one direction, and that was the first time that it was questioned and it sunk me, and it took years to get out of it.

What I will say though is that because of that, so many of the artists I work with deal with a lot of personal issues. And before that, before I was 33, I probably didn't understand how they felt as much as I do now, because now I've had an experience where I feel like the bottom fell out. I would say it has helped my understanding of the artists I work with.
